I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts loop, do you feel driven to check your work again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say it’s, um, out of control exactly. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but the thought keeps looping anyway. Then pretty soon I’ve gone from one little concern to another, and I’m checking the details in my head over and over. Sometimes I’ll look back at the work too, just to feel like I didn’t miss something. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you go back to check your work, does it make the uneasy feeling go down, even if only for a little while?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I think sometimes I feel a little better just knowing I looked, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether the uneasy feeling actually goes down or not. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you check your work again, are you doing it mainly to relieve a strong sense of anxiety or uneasiness?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, but I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is. It’s not like I’m panicking every time. It’s more that I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and the thought still keeps looping. Then I start going over one detail, then another, and before I know it I’m checking things in my head again and again, just trying to settle that uneasy feeling. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: About how much time in a typical day do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors take up?\n\nPatient: It’s not all day or anything. I’d say… maybe it adds up more than I realize, because I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and then the thought keeps looping. I go from one concern to another and end up checking little details in my head over and over.\n\nIf I had to guess, maybe a chunk here and there through the day—not hours and hours, I don’t think. Maybe around half an hour, sometimes more on a busy day? I’m not sure if that’s the kind of estimate you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors interfere with your work enough that you have trouble finishing tasks on time?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job. I am showing up and getting through the day.\n\nBut scheduling takes me longer than it used to, because I keep double-checking myself so much. And there have been times with the lobby displays where I lose track of what I was arranging and then have to redo parts of it. So… it can slow me down, I guess. Is that what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you may have missed something comes up, does part of you recognize that the worry is probably excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, part of me does know I’m probably making too much of it. Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? So I do recognize it’s maybe more worry than the situation really calls for… I just don’t always feel able to let it go right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Yes… not constantly, but it does happen outside of work too. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you check your work again, are you doing it mainly to relieve a strong sense of anxiety or uneasiness?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, but I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is. It’s not like I’m panicking every time. It’s more that I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and the thought still keeps looping. Then I start going over one detail, then another, and before I know it I’m checking things in my head again and again, just trying to settle that uneasy feeling. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: About how much time in a typical day do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors take up?\n\nPatient: It’s not all day or anything. I’d say… maybe it adds up more than I realize, because I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and then the thought keeps looping. I go from one concern to another and end up checking little details in my head over and over.\n\nIf I had to guess, maybe a chunk here and there through the day—not hours and hours, I don’t think. Maybe around half an hour, sometimes more on a busy day? I’m not sure if that’s the kind of estimate you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors interfere with your work enough that you have trouble finishing tasks on time?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job. I am showing up and getting through the day.\n\nBut scheduling takes me longer than it used to, because I keep double-checking myself so much. And there have been times with the lobby displays where I lose track of what I was arranging and then have to redo parts of it. So… it can slow me down, I guess. Is that what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you may have missed something comes up, does part of you recognize that the worry is probably excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, part of me does know I’m probably making too much of it. Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? So I do recognize it’s maybe more worry than the situation really calls for… I just don’t always feel able to let it go right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Yes… not constantly, but it does happen outside of work too. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? So I do recognize it’s maybe more worry than the situation really calls for… I just don’t always feel able to let it go right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Yes… not constantly, but it does happen outside of work too. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about many different areas of your life, even when you’re not having the thought that you may have missed something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s not only the “maybe I missed something” thought. I worry about a lot of normal, everyday things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes. Things other people probably just handle and move on from. But in my head it can feel like one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n\nI know that may be more worrying than necessary… but yes, it does happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about many different areas of your life, even when you’re not having the thought that you may have missed something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s not only the “maybe I missed something” thought. I worry about a lot of normal, everyday things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes. Things other people probably just handle and move on from. But in my head it can feel like one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n\nI know that may be more worrying than necessary… but yes, it does happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying, even when you try to stop or put it aside?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do try to stop it. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and sometimes that helps for a minute.\n\nBut then the thought kind of keeps looping anyway, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another, checking the details in my head over and over. I know that probably sounds a little silly… but yes, it can be hard for me to really put it aside.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess what brought me in is, um, it’s not anything too dramatic, but I’ve been noticing at work that I lose my place more than I used to. Like when I’m scheduling appointments, or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should ask about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do unwanted thoughts about having missed or overlooked something keep popping into your mind even when you try to move on?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not constantly in a dramatic way, but it does come back even after I’ve told myself, “Sunny, it’s handled.” Then the thought just sort of loops, and before I know it I’ve moved from one concern to another and I’m checking the details in my head over and over. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts loop, do you feel driven to check your work again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say it’s, um, out of control exactly. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but the thought keeps looping anyway. Then pretty soon I’ve gone from one little concern to another, and I’m checking the details in my head over and over. Sometimes I’ll look back at the work too, just to feel like I didn’t miss something. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you go back to check your work, does it make the uneasy feeling go down, even if only for a little while?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I think sometimes I feel a little better just knowing I looked, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether the uneasy feeling actually goes down or not. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you check your work again, are you doing it mainly to relieve a strong sense of anxiety or uneasiness?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, but I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is. It’s not like I’m panicking every time. It’s more that I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and the thought still keeps looping. Then I start going over one detail, then another, and before I know it I’m checking things in my head again and again, just trying to settle that uneasy feeling. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: About how much time in a typical day do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors take up?\n\nPatient: It’s not all day or anything. I’d say… maybe it adds up more than I realize, because I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and then the thought keeps looping. I go from one concern to another and end up checking little details in my head over and over.\n\nIf I had to guess, maybe a chunk here and there through the day—not hours and hours, I don’t think. Maybe around half an hour, sometimes more on a busy day? I’m not sure if that’s the kind of estimate you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors interfere with your work enough that you have trouble finishing tasks on time?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job. I am showing up and getting through the day.\n\nBut scheduling takes me longer than it used to, because I keep double-checking myself so much. Is that what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you may have missed something comes up, does part of you recognize that the worry is probably excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, part of me does know I’m probably making too much of it. Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about many different areas of your life, even when you’re not having the thought that you may have missed something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s not only the “maybe I missed something” thought. I worry about a lot of normal, everyday things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes. Things other people probably just handle and move on from. But in my head it can feel like one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n\nI know that may be more worrying than necessary… but yes, it does happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying, even when you try to stop or put it aside?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do try to stop it. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and sometimes that helps for a minute.\n\nBut then the thought kind of keeps looping anyway, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another, checking the details in my head over and over. I know that probably sounds a little silly… but yes, it can be hard for me to really put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not all the time, but pretty often.\n\nAt the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I don’t think most people would notice. But inside I can feel kind of wound tight… like my shoulders are up around my ears, and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nI know that maybe sounds dramatic. It’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s just this keyed-up feeling that’s there more than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just thought maybe I should ask about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do unwanted thoughts about having missed or overlooked something keep popping into your mind even when you try to move on?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not constantly in a dramatic way, but it does come back even after I’ve told myself, “Sunny, it’s handled.” Then the thought just sort of loops, and before I know it I’ve moved from one concern to another and I’m checking the details in my head over and over. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts loop, do you feel driven to check your work again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say it’s, um, out of control exactly. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but the thought keeps looping anyway. Then pretty soon I’ve gone from one little concern to another, and I’m checking the details in my head over and over. Sometimes I’ll look back at the work too, just to feel like I didn’t miss something. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you go back to check your work, does it make the uneasy feeling go down, even if only for a little while?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Is that what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you may have missed something comes up, does part of you recognize that the worry is probably excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, part of me does know I’m probably making too much of it. Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? So I do recognize it’s maybe more worry than the situation really calls for… I just don’t always feel able to let it go right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Yes… not constantly, but it does happen outside of work too. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about many different areas of your life, even when you’re not having the thought that you may have missed something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s not only the “maybe I missed something” thought. I worry about a lot of normal, everyday things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes. Things other people probably just handle and move on from. But in my head it can feel like one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n\nI know that may be more worrying than necessary… but yes, it does happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying, even when you try to stop or put it aside?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do try to stop it. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and sometimes that helps for a minute.\n\nBut then the thought kind of keeps looping anyway, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another, checking the details in my head over and over. I know that probably sounds a little silly… but yes, it can be hard for me to really put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not all the time, but pretty often.\n\nAt the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I don’t think most people would notice. But inside I can feel kind of wound tight… like my shoulders are up around my ears, and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nI know that maybe sounds dramatic. It’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s just this keyed-up feeling that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say I’m tense every second or anything.\n\nIt’s more like even when things are quiet, I have trouble fully settling. At the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I think I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up near my ears and I’m kind of waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nDoes that count as trouble relaxing?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’d say… maybe it adds up more than I realize, because I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and then the thought keeps looping. I go from one concern to another and end up checking little details in my head over and over.\n\nIf I had to guess, maybe a chunk here and there through the day—not hours and hours, I don’t think. Maybe around half an hour, sometimes more on a busy day? I’m not sure if that’s the kind of estimate you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors interfere with your work enough that you have trouble finishing tasks on time?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job. I am showing up and getting through the day.\n\nBut scheduling takes me longer than it used to, because I keep double-checking myself so much. And there have been times with the lobby displays where I lose track of what I was arranging and then have to redo parts of it. So… it can slow me down, I guess. Is that what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you may have missed something comes up, does part of you recognize that the worry is probably excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, part of me does know I’m probably making too much of it. Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? So I do recognize it’s maybe more worry than the situation really calls for… I just don’t always feel able to let it go right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Yes… not constantly, but it does happen outside of work too. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about many different areas of your life, even when you’re not having the thought that you may have missed something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s not only the “maybe I missed something” thought. I worry about a lot of normal, everyday things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes. Things other people probably just handle and move on from. But in my head it can feel like one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n\nI know that may be more worrying than necessary… but yes, it does happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying, even when you try to stop or put it aside?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do try to stop it. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and sometimes that helps for a minute.\n\nBut then the thought kind of keeps looping anyway, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another, checking the details in my head over and over. I know that probably sounds a little silly… but yes, it can be hard for me to really put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not all the time, but pretty often.\n\nAt the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I don’t think most people would notice. But inside I can feel kind of wound tight… like my shoulders are up around my ears, and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nI know that maybe sounds dramatic. It’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s just this keyed-up feeling that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say I’m tense every second or anything.\n\nIt’s more like even when things are quiet, I have trouble fully settling. At the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I think I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up near my ears and I’m kind of waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nDoes that count as trouble relaxing?\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been about nine months now, I guess.\n\nIt started after that overnight detention and the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but since then the worrying has been there most days. Does that fit what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like when I’m scheduling appointments, or even working on the lobby displays, my mind kind of slips off track because I’m thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked. I just thought maybe I should ask about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do unwanted thoughts about having missed or overlooked something keep popping into your mind even when you try to move on?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not constantly in a dramatic way, but it does come back even after I’ve told myself, “Sunny, it’s handled.” Then the thought just sort of loops, and before I know it I’ve moved from one concern to another and I’m checking the details in my head over and over. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts loop, do you feel driven to check your work again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say it’s, um, out of control exactly. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but the thought keeps looping anyway. Then pretty soon I’ve gone from one little concern to another, and I’m checking the details in my head over and over. Sometimes I’ll look back at the work too, just to feel like I didn’t miss something. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you go back to check your work, does it make the uneasy feeling go down, even if only for a little while?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I think sometimes I feel a little better just knowing I looked, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether the uneasy feeling actually goes down or not. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you check your work again, are you doing it mainly to relieve a strong sense of anxiety or uneasiness?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, but I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is. It’s not like I’m panicking every time. It’s more that I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and the thought still keeps looping. Then I start going over one detail, then another, and before I know it I’m checking things in my head again and again, just trying to settle that uneasy feeling. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: About how much time in a typical day do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors take up?\n\nPatient: It’s not all day or anything. I’d say… maybe it adds up more than I realize, because I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and then the thought keeps looping. I go from one concern to another and end up checking little details in my head over and over.\n\nIf I had to guess, maybe a chunk here and there through the day—not hours and hours, I don’t think. Maybe around half an hour, sometimes more on a busy day? I’m not sure if that’s the kind of estimate you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do these checking thoughts and rechecking behaviors interfere with your work enough that you have trouble finishing tasks on time?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job. I am showing up and getting through the day.\n\nBut scheduling takes me longer than it used to, because I keep double-checking myself so much. And there have been times with the lobby displays where I lose track of what I was arranging and then have to redo parts of it. So… it can slow me down, I guess. Is that what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you may have missed something comes up, does part of you recognize that the worry is probably excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, part of me does know I’m probably making too much of it. Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? So I do recognize it’s maybe more worry than the situation really calls for… I just don’t always feel able to let it go right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Yes… not constantly, but it does happen outside of work too. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about many different areas of your life, even when you’re not having the thought that you may have missed something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s not only the “maybe I missed something” thought. I worry about a lot of normal, everyday things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes. Things other people probably just handle and move on from. But in my head it can feel like one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n\nI know that may be more worrying than necessary… but yes, it does happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying, even when you try to stop or put it aside?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do try to stop it. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and sometimes that helps for a minute.\n\nBut then the thought kind of keeps looping anyway, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another, checking the details in my head over and over. I know that probably sounds a little silly… but yes, it can be hard for me to really put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not all the time, but pretty often.\n\nAt the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I don’t think most people would notice. But inside I can feel kind of wound tight… like my shoulders are up around my ears, and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nI know that maybe sounds dramatic. It’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s just this keyed-up feeling that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say I’m tense every second or anything.\n\nIt’s more like even when things are quiet, I have trouble fully settling. At the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I think I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up near my ears and I’m kind of waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nDoes that count as trouble relaxing?\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been about nine months now, I guess.\n\nIt started after that overnight detention and the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but since then the worrying has been there most days. Does that fit what you mean?\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, about how much total time do you spend checking or rechecking things, either in your head or by going back to look again?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s hard to put an exact number on it, because a lot of it is in my head, not me physically going back to look.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but then the thought just keeps looping. And pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another, and I’m checking details over and over in my mind. I don’t want to exaggerate it… but it probably adds up more than I’d like during the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that what you mean by interfering?\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you may have missed something comes up, does part of you recognize that the worry is probably excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, part of me does know I’m probably making too much of it. Like with work schedules or a bill or travel plans, even some small paperwork thing—I’ll think, “Okay, realistically, this is probably fine.”\n\nBut then another part of me starts going, what if one little mistake turns into a bigger problem? So I do recognize it’s maybe more worry than the situation really calls for… I just don’t always feel able to let it go right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Yes… not constantly, but it does happen outside of work too. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do know that for a second, but then the thought keeps looping.\n\nAnd then pretty soon I’ve moved from one thing to another, just checking details in my head over and over. I don’t know if that counts as “repeatedly checking” in the way you mean, but… yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to check things even when you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t really want to keep checking, and I know it probably sounds silly, but I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a moment that helps.\n\nThen the thought just keeps looping, like maybe I missed one detail. And before I know it, I’ve gone from that one concern to another one, checking things over in my head again and again. Not because I want to, exactly… more because it’s hard to settle unless I do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: When you check something, do you often go back and check the same thing more than once?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t think it’s, like, a huge dramatic thing, but I can go back to the same detail more than once in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and I do believe that for a moment, but then the thought keeps looping. Then pretty soon I’m checking that same thing again, or I’ve moved on to another concern and I’m checking those details too. I know it’s probably more than I need to do… I just have trouble letting it rest sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about having missed something feel intrusive and unwanted when they pop into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… unwanted, I think. I don’t sit there trying to have the thought, if that makes sense.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, we’re good. But then it pops back up and kind of keeps looping, like maybe I missed something. And then I’m going from one concern to another, checking the details in my head again.\n\nIt’s not like I want to be doing that… it just feels hard to shut off sometimes. Is that what you mean by intrusive?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about many different areas of your life, even when you’re not having the thought that you may have missed something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s not only the “maybe I missed something” thought. I worry about a lot of normal, everyday things—my work schedule, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes. Things other people probably just handle and move on from. But in my head it can feel like one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n\nI know that may be more worrying than necessary… but yes, it does happen. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying, even when you try to stop or put it aside?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do try to stop it. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and sometimes that helps for a minute.\n\nBut then the thought kind of keeps looping anyway, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another, checking the details in my head over and over. I know that probably sounds a little silly… but yes, it can be hard for me to really put it aside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not all the time, but pretty often.\n\nAt the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I don’t think most people would notice. But inside I can feel kind of wound tight… like my shoulders are up around my ears, and I’m just waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nI know that maybe sounds dramatic. It’s not like I’m falling apart or anything. It’s just this keyed-up feeling that’s there more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I wouldn’t say I’m tense every second or anything.\n\nIt’s more like even when things are quiet, I have trouble fully settling. At the front desk I can still smile and help people, so I think I probably look fine. But inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up near my ears and I’m kind of waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nDoes that count as trouble relaxing?\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been about nine months now, I guess.\n\nIt started after that overnight detention and the paperwork mix-up around it. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but since then the worrying has been there most days. Does that fit what you mean?\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, about how much total time do you spend checking or rechecking things, either in your head or by going back to look again?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s hard to put an exact number on it, because a lot of it is in my head, not me physically going back to look.\n\nI can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” but then the thought just keeps looping. And pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another, and I’m checking details over and over in my mind. I don’t want to exaggerate it… but it probably adds up more than I’d like during the day.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, do these checking thoughts and rechecking habits take more than an hour altogether?\n\nPatient: I… I think on some days, yes, it probably does add up to more than an hour. I don’t sit there with a timer or anything, and I’m still getting my work done, but it’s that looping in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and then the thought comes back anyway. Then I’m checking one detail, then another, and before I know it I’ve gone over the same things in my mind several times. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s the kind of checking you mean, but… yes, altogether it can be more than an hour.\n\nDoctor: When you go over details in your mind, does it feel like you have to do it in a certain way until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it has to be in a certain way, exactly. It’s more like… I keep turning it over because I don’t feel settled yet.\n\nBut I don’t have a ritual or a specific order I have to follow, if that’s what you mean. I just want to feel sure I didn’t miss something. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
